Cheap Permutation Testing
Carles Domingo-Enrichlabel=e1]carlesd@microsoft.com, Raaz Dwivedilabel=e2]dwivedi@cornell.edu, Lester Mackeylabel=e3]lmackey@microsoft.com

Abstract
Permutation tests are a popular choice for distinguishing distributions and testing independence, due to their exact, finite-sample control of false positives and their minimax optimality when paired with U-statistics. However, standard permutation tests are also expensive, requiring a test statistic to be computed hundreds or thousands of times to detect a separation between distributions. In this work, we offer a simple approach to accelerate testing: group your datapoints into bins and permute the bin labels. For U and V-statistics, we prove that these cheap permutation tests have two remarkable properties. First, by storing appropriate sufficient statistics, a cheap test can be run in time comparable to evaluating a single test statistic. Second, cheap permutation power closely approximates standard permutation power. As a result, cheap tests inherit the exact false positive control and minimax optimality of standard permutation tests while running in a fraction of the time. We complement these findings with improved power guarantees for standard permutation testing and experiments demonstrating the benefits of cheap permutations over standard maximum mean discrepancy (MMD), Hilbert-Schmidt independence criterion (HSIC), random Fourier feature, Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney, aggregated MMD, cross-MMD, and cross-HSIC tests.

1
Introduction

Permutation tests
[
20
,
17
]
are commonly used in statistics, machine learning, and the sciences
to test for independence,
detect distributional discrepancies,
evaluate fairness, and
assess model quality
[
22
,
48
,
15
,
39
]
.
By simply permuting the labels assigned to datapoints, these tests offer both exact, non-asymptotic control over false positives
[
28
]
and minimax optimal error rates when paired with popular U and V test statistics
[
31
,
5
,
52
,
51
]
.

However, standard permutation tests are also expensive,
requiring the recomputation of a test statistic hundreds or thousands of times.
This work offers a simple remedy:
divide your data into
s
bins and permute the bin labels.
In
Sec.

4
we show that this
cheap permutation
strategy recovers the best known error rates for U and V-statistic permutation testing, even when the bin count
s
is independent of the sample size
n
.
Moreover, we establish in
Sec.

3
that, after computing the initial test statistic, the same cheap tests can be run in time independent of
n
by keeping track of simple sufficient statistics.
Together, these findings give rise to new exact tests that inherit the minimax rate optimality of standard permutation testing
while running in
time comparable to the cost of a single test statistic.

1.1
Related work

A number of approaches to speeding up permutation testing have appeared in the literature.

Zmigrod, Vieira and Cotterell [65]
showed that, for a special class of
G
-bounded, additively-decomposable, and integer-valued test statistics, one can run an independence permutation test using all
n!
distinct permutations of
n
points in time
O(Gn\log(Gn)\log(n))
.
Unfortunately, this class does not cover the more general U and V-statistics studied in this work.

For large sample sizes, one typically avoids the prohibitive cost of enumerating
n!
permutations by instead iterating over a small subgroup of permutations
[
9
]
or sampling
B
permutations uniformly at random
[
17
]
.
In
Sec.

3
, we establish a complementary cost savings for binned permutations and quadratic test statistics: each bin-permuted statistic can be computed in time independent of the sample size.

Recently,
Koning and Hemerik [36]
,
Koning [35]
revealed another surprising benefit of restricted permutation: certain small subgroups yield greater power than testing with the full permutation group.
Their analysis focuses on the case of generalized location models, and an interesting open question is whether those gains transfer to the more general settings and subgroups studied in this work.

In the context of homogeneity testing,
Chung et al. [12]

replaced random permutations with transpositions of random pairs of elements showing that, for certain test statistics,
each transposed statistic could be computed in time independent of the sample size
n
.
However, for the higher-order U and V-statistics studied in this work, the cost of computing a transposed statistic grows linearly in
n
, while our
cheap permutation statistics can still be computed in time independent of
n
.
In addition,
Chung et al. [12]
did not analyze the power of their proposed test.

Domingo-Enrich, Dwivedi and Mackey [16]
used binned permutation in combination with sample compression to reduce the runtime of homogeneity testing with a bounded kernel maximum mean discrepancy
[
25
, MMD,]
test statistic.
Here we show that binned permutation provides benefits for a much broader range of tests, including independence tests and homogeneity tests based on general quadratic test statistics (see
Def.

1
).

Anderson and Verbeeck [1]

computed the exact mean and variance of the permutation distribution for a class of multivariate generalizations of the Mann-Whitney test called generalized pairwise comparison (GPC) statistics. However, the authors noted, “In order to construct a hypothesis test for the GPC statistics, any use of the means and variances would require the assumption of asymptotic normality,” an assumption which is not needed for our proposed methodology.
Relatedly, a wide variety of works accelerate testing by fitting an approximation to the permutation distribution
[
64
,
34
,
37
,
53
,
26
, see, e.g.,]
.
However, these works do not analyze the power of their approximate tests, and, in each case, the approximation sacrifices finite-sample validity.

Sequential permutation testing
[
6
,
18
,
58
,
19
, see, e.g.,]
reduces the total number of permutations required for a powerful test by processing permuted statistics sequentially and applying an early stopping rule.
This strategy normally incurs the full cost of recomputing a test statistic for each permutation but can be combined with cheap permutation statistics to yield testing overhead independent of the sample size.

Ramdas et al. [50]

established the
validity
of a wide variety of generalized permutation tests (involving arbitrary subsets of the permutation distribution and nonuniform distributions over them). However, their work does not discuss power or computational complexity, two matters of primary importance in the present work.

Finally, the pioneering work of
Kim, Balakrishnan and Wasserman [31]
established strong finite-sample power guarantees and minimax rate-optimality results for standard U-statistic permutation tests with
B
permutations and order
\Theta(n^{2}+Bn^{2})
runtime. Perhaps surprisingly, we find that binned permutation tests enjoy equivalent power guarantees and minimax rate optimality even when the bin count
s
is independent of the sample size. Since bin-permuted U-statistics can be computed with only
O(s^{2})
overhead, this opens the door to powerful exact testing in
O(n^{2}+Bs^{2})
time, that is, in time comparable to computing a single test statistic.

1.2
Contributions

The primary contribution of this work lies in the development of practical cheap testing procedures that, on the one hand, dramatically reduce the computational requirements of permutation testing and, on the other, retain the high-quality power properties of standard permutation testing both in theory and in practice. More precisely:

1.

We develop cheap tests of homogeneity and independence with only
Bs^{2}
permutation overhead thanks to binned permutation and compact sufficient statistics.

2.

We adapt the arguments of
Kim, Balakrishnan and Wasserman [31]
and
Domingo-Enrich, Dwivedi and Mackey [16]
to develop quantitative power guarantees for cheap permutation testing with U and V-statistics. Remarkably, the guarantees for
s
bins closely match the standard guarantees for
n
bins, even when the bin count is independent of
n
.

3.

Sec.

5
builds on these results to establish the minimax rate-optimality of cheap permutation tests for the four classic testing problems studied by
Kim, Balakrishnan and Wasserman [31]
:
discrete homogeneity testing, Hölder homogeneity testing, discrete independence testing, and Hölder independence testing. Notably, this optimality is achievable with a bin count and hence a permutation time independent of the sample size.

4.

Moving beyond the classic testing settings, we use the lower bounds of
Kim and Schrab [32]
to establish the minimax-rate optimality of cheap testing for (a) MMD homogeneity testing and (b) Hilbert-Schmidt independence criterion
[
23
, HSIC,]
independence testing. This optimality is again achievable with a bin count independent of
n
.

5.

In
Sec.

6
, we complement these findings with experiments demonstrating the practical benefits of cheap testing over standard MMD, HSIC, random Fourier feature
[
49
]
, Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney
[
63
,
41
]
, aggregated MMD, cross-MMD
[
56
]
, and cross-HSIC tests
[
57
]
.

6.

Finally, we outline the extension of cheap testing to broader testing problems and test statistics in
Sec.

7
.

1.3
Notation

Given a permutation
\pi
of the indices
[n]\triangleq\{1,\dots,n\}
and an index
i\in[n]
, we write either
\pi(i)
or
\pi_{i}
to denote the index to which
i
is mapped.
For integers
p\geq q\geq 1
, we define
(p)_{q}\triangleq p(p-1)\cdots{(p-q+1)}
and
let
\mathbf{i}_{q}^{p}
denote the set of all
q
-tuples that can be drawn without replacement from the set
[p]
.
For a function
g
integrable under a probability measure
\mathbb{P}
, we use
the notation
\mathbb{P}g\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathbb{P}}[g(X)]
.
We use
\mathbf{1}
for the indicator function.
For two nonnegative sequences
(a_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
(b_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
, we write
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
if there exists a constant
C>0
such that
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
for all sufficiently large
n
and
a_{n}=\Omega(b_{n})
if
b_{n}=O(a_{n})
. We write
a_{n}=\Theta(b_{n})
if both
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
and
a_{n}=\Omega(b_{n})
and write
a_{n}=\omega(b_{n})
if
a_{n}/b_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
.

2
Background on hypothesis testing

We define a test as any measurable procedure that takes as input a dataset
\mathbb{X}\triangleq(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
and outputs a probability in
[0,1]
.
Given two competing hypotheses (
\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}
) concerning the data generating distribution, we interpret
\Delta(\mathbb{X})
as the probability of rejecting the
null hypothesis

\mathcal{H}_{0}
in favor of the
alternative hypothesis

\mathcal{H}_{1}
.
The quality of a test is measured by
its
size
, its probability of (incorrectly) rejecting the null when the null is true, and its
power
, its probability of (correctly) rejecting
\mathcal{H}_{0}
when
\mathcal{H}_{1}
is true.
A test often comes paired with a
nominal level
, a desired upper bound
\alpha\in(0,1)
on the Type I error.
We say
\Delta
is a
valid level-
\alpha
test if its size never exceeds
\alpha
when
\mathcal{H}_{0}
holds and an
exact level-
\alpha
test if its size exactly equals
\alpha
whenever
\mathcal{H}_{0}
holds.
All of the tests studied in this work are exact and take the form

\textstyle\Delta(\mathbb{X})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }T(\mathbb{X})>c(\mathbb{X}),\\
\xi(\mathbb{X})&\text{if }T(\mathbb{X})=c(\mathbb{X}),\text{ and }\\
0&\text{if }T(\mathbb{X})<c(\mathbb{X}),\\
\end{cases}

(2)

where
T(\mathbb{X})
is called the
test statistic
,
c(\mathbb{X})
is called the
critical value
, and
\xi(\mathbb{X})
is a rejection probability chosen to ensure exactness.
We will consider two broad classes of tests in this work: tests of homogeneity and tests of independence.

2.1
Homogeneity testing

Homogeneity tests
(also known as
two-sample tests
) test for equality of distribution.
More precisely, given a sample
\mathbb{Y}=(Y_{i})_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
drawn i.i.d. from an unknown distribution
\mathbb{P}
and an independent sample
\mathbb{Z}=(Z_{j})_{j=1}^{n_{2}}
drawn i.i.d. from an unknown distribution
\mathbb{Q}
, a homogeneity test judges the hypotheses

\textstyle\mathcal{H}_{0}:\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{Q}\quad\text{versus}\quad\mathcal{H}_{1}:\mathbb{P}\neq\mathbb{Q}.

(3)

A common approach to testing homogeneity is to threshold a
quadratic test statistic (QTS)
.

Definition 1

(Quadratic test statistic (QTS))
.

Given samples
\mathbb{Y}
and
\mathbb{Z}
of size
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
respectively, we call
T(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})
a
quadratic test statistic
, if
T
is a quadratic form of the empirical distributions
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\triangleq\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Y}}\delta_{y}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\triangleq\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}\delta_{z}
. That is, there exist base functions
\phi_{n_{1}},\phi_{n_{2}}
, and
\phi_{n_{1},n_{2}}
for which
T
satisfies

\textstyle T(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})

\textstyle=(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{P}})\phi_{n_{1}}+(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Q}})\phi_{n_{1},n_{2}}+(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Q}})\phi_{n_{2}}.

(4)

An important example of a QTS the canonical
homogeneity U-statistic
.

Definition 2

(Homogeneity U-statistic)
.

Given samples
\mathbb{Y}
and
\mathbb{Z}
of size
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
respectively, we call
U(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})
, or
U_{n_{1},n_{2}}
for short, a
homogeneity U-statistic
if

\textstyle U_{n_{1},n_{2}}

\textstyle=\frac{1}{(n_{1})_{2}(n_{2})_{2}}\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n_{1}}}\sum_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{n_{2}}}h_{\mathrm{ho}}(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}};Z_{j_{1}},Z_{j_{2}}),\quad\text{and}\quad

(5)

\textstyle h_{\mathrm{ho}}(y_{1},y_{2};z_{1},z_{2})

\textstyle=g(y_{1},y_{2})+g(z_{1},z_{2})-g(y_{1},z_{2})-g(y_{2},z_{1})

(6)

for some symmetric
real-valued base function
g
.

Notably, any homogeneity U-statistic can be written as a QTS
4
via the mappings

\textstyle\phi_{w}(y,z)

\textstyle=\frac{1}{1-1/w}(g(y,z)-\frac{g(y,y)+g(z,z)}{w+1})\text{ for }w\in\{n_{1},n_{2}\}\text{ and }\phi_{n_{1},n_{2}}(y,z)=-2g(y,z).

(7)

Moreover, many commonly used test statistics can be recovered as homogeneity U-statistics with the right choice of
g
.

Example 1

(Kernel two-sample test statistics
[
25
]
)
.

When
g
is chosen to be a positive-definite kernel
\mathbf{k}

[
59
, Def. 4.15]
, then
U_{n_{1},n_{2}}
(
5
) is a
kernel two-sample test statistic

and an unbiased estimator of the squared
maximum mean discrepancy (MMD)
between the distributions
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
:

\textstyle\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})

\textstyle\triangleq(\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P})\mathbf{k}+(\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q})\mathbf{k}-2(\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q})\mathbf{k}.

(8)

Example 2

(Energy statistics
[
4
,
61
,
54
]
)
.

When
g
is the negative Euclidean distance on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
U_{n_{1},n_{2}}

5
is called an
energy statistic
.
More generally, if
g(y,z)=-\rho(y,z)
for a semimetric
\rho
of negative type
[
54
, Defs. 1-2]
, then
U_{n_{1},n_{2}}
is a
generalized energy statistic
and an unbiased estimate of the
energy distance
between
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
:

\textstyle\mathcal{E}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=2(\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q})\rho-(\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P})\rho-(\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q})\rho.

(9)

In both of these examples,
the homogeneity U-statistic
5
is degenerate under the null with
(n_{1}+n_{2})\,U_{n_{1},n_{2}}
converging to a complicated,
\mathbb{P}
-dependent, non-Gaussian limit as
n_{1},n_{2}\to\infty

[
55
, Sec. 5.5.2]
.
As a result, one typically
turns to
permutation testing, as described in
Sec.

3.1
, to
select the critical values for
these statistics
[
60
,
40
,
61
,
4
]
.

2.2
Independence testing

Independence tests
test for dependence between paired variables.
More precisely, given a paired sample
(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})\triangleq(Y_{i},Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
drawn
i.i.d. from an unknown distribution
\Pi
with marginals
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, independence testing judges the hypotheses

\textstyle\mathcal{H}_{0}:\Pi=\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q}\quad\text{versus}\quad\mathcal{H}_{1}:\Pi\neq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q}.

(10)

A common approach to testing independence is to threshold an
independence V-statistic
.

Definition 3

(Independence V-statistic)
.

Given a paired sample
(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})
of size
n
, we call
V(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})
, or
V_{n}
for short, an
independence V-statistic
if

\textstyle V_{n}

\textstyle=\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in[n]^{4}}h_{\mathrm{in}}((Y_{i_{1}},Z_{i_{1}}),(Y_{i_{2}},Z_{i_{2}}),(Y_{i_{3}},Z_{i_{3}}),(Y_{i_{4}},Z_{i_{4}})),\quad\text{for}\quad

(11)

\textstyle h_{\mathrm{in}}

\textstyle((y_{1},z_{1}),(y_{2},z_{2}),(y_{3},z_{3}),(y_{4},z_{4}))

(12)

\textstyle\triangleq\big(g_{Y}(y_{1},y_{2})+g_{Y}(y_{3},y_{4})-g_{Y}(y_{1},y_{3})-g_{Y}(y_{2},y_{4})\big)

(13)

\textstyle\quad\times\big(g_{Z}(z_{1},z_{2})+g_{Z}(z_{3},z_{4})-g_{Z}(z_{1},z_{3})-g_{Z}(z_{2},z_{4})\big).

(14)

for some symmetric real-valued base functions
g_{Y}
and
g_{Z}
.

Many commonly used test statistics can be recovered as independence V-statistics with the right choices of
g_{Y}
and
g_{Z}
.

Example 3

(Hilbert-Schmidt independence criterion (HSIC)
[
23
]
)
.

When
g_{Y}
and
g_{Z}
are positive-definite kernels,
\frac{1}{4}V_{n}

11
is the empirical
Hilbert-Schmidt independence criterion
, i.e., the squared maximum mean discrepancy
\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}(\widehat{\Pi},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Q}})

8
,
where
\mathbf{k}=g_{Y}g_{Z}
,

\widehat{\Pi}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{(Y_{i},Z_{i})}
,
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{Y_{i}}
, and
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{Z_{i}}

[
51
, App. B]
.

Example 4

(Distance covariance
[
62
,
54
]
)
.

When
g_{Y}(y_{1},y_{2})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{y_{1}-y_{2}}}}\right\|_{2}
and
g_{Z}(z_{1},z_{2})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{z_{1}-z_{2}}}}\right\|_{2}
for
y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
and
z_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
,
\frac{1}{4}V_{n}

11
is called the
distance covariance
and forms the basis for the
energy test of independence
. More generally, if
g_{Y}
and
g_{Z}
are semimetrics of negative type
[
54
, Defs. 1-2]
, then
V_{n}
is a
generalized distance covariance
.

In both of these examples, the independence V-statistic
12
is degenerate under the null with
nV_{n}
converging to a complicated,
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
-dependent, non-Gaussian limit as
n\to\infty

[
55
, Sec. 5.5.2]
.
As a result, one typically turns to permutation testing, for example, as described in
Sec.

3.3
, to select critical values for these statistics
[
62
,
13
]
.

3
Cheap permutation testing

Given a test statistic, permutation testing provides an automated procedure for selecting the critical value and rejection probability of a test
2
.
In this section, we review standard (Monte Carlo) permutation tests for homogeneity and independence and introduce new cheap permutation tests that reduce the runtime and memory demands. The results of this section are summarized in
Tab.

1
.

3.1
Permutation tests of homogeneity

Let

\mathbb{X}\triangleq(X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n_{1}},Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n_{2}})

represent a concatenation of the observed samples
\mathbb{Y}=(Y_{i})_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
and
\mathbb{Z}=(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n_{2}}
with

n\triangleq n_{1}+n_{2}
.
In a standard permutation test of homogeneity, one
samples
B
independent permutations
(\pi_{b})_{b=1}^{B}
of the indices
[n]
,
computes the permuted test statistics
(T(\mathbb{X}^{\pi_{b}}))_{b=0}^{B}

for
\pi_{0}
the identity permutation and

\mathbb{X}^{\pi}\triangleq(X_{\pi(i)})_{i=1}^{n}
,
and finally selects a
1-\alpha
quantile of the empirical distribution
\frac{1}{B+1}\sum_{b=0}^{B}\delta_{T(\mathbb{X}^{\pi_{b}})}
as the critical test value
c(\mathbb{X})
.

For a quadratic test statistic (
Def.

1
), this permutation test only depends on the data via the
3n^{2}
sufficient statistics

\textstyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ho}}\triangleq\big\{\phi(x_{i},x_{j})\mid i,j\in[n],\phi\in\{\phi_{n_{1}},\phi_{n_{2}},\phi_{n_{1},n_{2}}\}\big\}.

(15)

Hence, if we let
c_{\phi}
denote the maximum time required to evaluate a single element of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ho}}
, then one can precompute and store the elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ho}}
given
\Theta(c_{\phi}n^{2})
time and
\Theta(n^{2})
memory and, thereafter, compute all permuted test statistics using
\Theta(Bn^{2})
elementary operations.
When the quadratic memory cost is unsupportable, the elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ho}}
can instead be recomputed for each permutation at a total cost of
\Theta(c_{\phi}Bn^{2})
time and
\Theta(n)
memory.

3.2
Cheap permutation tests of homogeneity

To alleviate both the time and memory burden of standard permutation testing, we propose to permute datapoint bins instead individual points.
More precisely, we partition the indices
[n]
into
s
consecutive sets
\mathcal{I}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{I}_{s}
of equal size,
identify the corresponding datapoint bins
\mathbb{X}_{j}\triangleq(X_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}
, and define the bin-permuted samples
\mathbb{Y}^{\pi}\triangleq(\mathbb{X}_{\pi(i)})_{i=1}^{s_{1}}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}\triangleq(\mathbb{X}_{\pi(i)})_{s_{1}+1}^{s}
for each permutation
\pi
on
[s]
and
s_{1}\triangleq s\frac{n_{1}}{n}
.
Importantly, the bin-permuted QTS,

\textstyle T(\mathbb{Y}^{\pi},\mathbb{Z}^{\pi})

\textstyle=\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{s_{1}}\Phi^{n_{1}}_{ij}+\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}\sum_{j=s_{1}+1}^{s}\Phi^{n_{1},n_{2}}_{ij}+\frac{1}{n_{2}^{2}}\sum_{i,j=s_{1}+1}^{s}\Phi^{n_{2}}_{ij},

(16)

only depends on the data via the
3s^{2}
sufficient statistics

\textstyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ho},\textrm{cheap}}\triangleq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\Phi^{w}_{ij}\triangleq\sum_{a\in\mathcal{I}_{i}}\sum_{b\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}\phi_{w}(X_{a},X_{b})\mid i,j\in[s],w\in\{n_{1},n_{2},(n_{1},n_{2})\}}}\right\}.

(17)

Hence, one can precompute and store the elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ho},\textrm{cheap}}
using only
\Theta(s^{2})
memory and
\Theta(c_{\phi}n^{2})
time and subsequently compute all bin-permuted statistics using only
\Theta(Bs^{2})
elementary operations.

Alg.

1

details the steps of this
cheap homogeneity test
which recovers the standard permutation test when
s=n
.

Notably, the overhead of cheap permutation is negligible whenever
Bs^{2}\ll c_{\phi}n^{2}
.
In
Secs.

4
and
6
, we will see that it suffices to choose
s
as a slow-growing or even constant function of
n
,
yielding total runtime comparable to that of evaluating the original test statistic.

Table 1:
Time and memory costs for cheap and standard permutation tests with
n
datapoints,
B
permutations, and
s
bins. The multipliers
c_{\phi}
and
c_{g}
denote the cost of evaluating a single homogeneity QTS base function (see
Def.

1
) and a single independence V-statistic base function (see
Def.

3
).
All costs are reported up to a universal constant.

Permutation test

Test statistic
\boldsymbol{T(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})}

Algorithm 1

Cheap homogeneity testing

Input:

Samples
(Y_{i})_{i=1}^{n_{1}}
,
(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n_{2}}
, bin count
s
, base functions
\phi_{n_{1}},\phi_{n_{2}},\phi_{n_{1},n_{2}}
(
Def.

1
), level
\alpha
, permutation count
B

1

Define
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\triangleq(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n_{1}},Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n_{2}})
for
n\leftarrow n_{1}+n_{2}
and
s_{1}\leftarrow{s\,n_{1}/}{n}

2

Split the indices
\{1,\ldots,n\}
into
s
consecutive bins
\mathcal{I}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{I}_{s}
of equal size

3

// Compute sufficient statistics using
\Theta(c_{\phi}n^{2})
time and
\Theta(s^{2})
memory

4

for

i,j\in[s]
and
w\in\{n_{1},n_{2},(n_{1},n_{2})\}

do

\Phi^{w}_{ij}\leftarrow\sum_{a\in\mathcal{I}_{i},b\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}\phi_{w}(X_{a},X_{b})

;

5

// Compute original and permuted test statistics using
\Theta(Bs^{2})
elementary operations

6

for

b=0,1,2,\ldots,B

do

7

\pi\leftarrow
identity permutation if
b=0
else uniform permutation of
[s]

8

T_{b}\leftarrow\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{s_{1}}\Phi^{n_{1}}_{\pi(i)\pi(j)}+\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{s_{1}}\sum_{j=s_{1}+1}^{s}\Phi^{n_{1},n_{2}}_{\pi(i)\pi(j)}

+\frac{1}{n_{2}^{2}}\sum_{i,j=s_{1}+1}^{s}\Phi^{n_{2}}_{\pi(i)\pi(j)}

9

end for

10

// Return rejection probability

11

R\leftarrow 1+
number of permuted statistics
(T_{b})_{b=1}^{B}

smaller than
T_{0}
if ties are broken at random

12

return

\Delta(\mathbb{X})\triangleq\min(1,\max(0,R\!-\!(1\!-\!\alpha)(B\!+\!1)))

3.3
Wild bootstrap tests of independence

To ease notation, we will focus on the case of even
n
.
In a standard wild bootstrap permutation test of independence
[
13
]
, one begins by sampling

B
independent
wild bootstrap permutations

(\pi_{b})_{b=1}^{B}
of the indices
[n]
.
Each wild bootstrap permutation
\pi
swaps the indices

i
and

\texttt{swap}(i)\triangleq i+\frac{n}{2}-n\mathbf{1}_{i>\frac{n}{2}}

with probability
\frac{1}{2}
and does so independently for each
i\leq n/2
.
Then, one computes the permuted test statistics
(T(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z}^{\pi_{b}}))_{b=0}^{B}

for
\pi_{0}
the identity permutation and

\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}\triangleq(Z_{\pi(i)})_{i=1}^{n}

and finally selects a
1-\alpha
quantile of the empirical distribution
\frac{1}{B+1}\sum_{b=0}^{B}\delta_{T(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z}^{\pi_{b}})}
as the critical test value
c(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})
.

Now consider the independence V-statistic
V(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})
of
Def.

3
, and introduce the shorthand

\textstyle(\bar{g}_{Y,i},\,\bar{g}_{Z,i},\,\bar{g}_{Y},\,\bar{g}_{Z})

\textstyle\triangleq(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}g_{Y}(Y_{i},Y_{j}),\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}g_{Z}(Z_{i},Z_{j}),\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{g}_{Y,i},\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{g}_{Z,i})

(18)

\textstyle\quad\text{and}\quad G_{ij,k\ell}

\textstyle\triangleq(g_{Y}(Y_{i},Y_{j})-\bar{g}_{Y,i}-\bar{g}_{Y,j}+\bar{g}_{Y})(g_{Z}(Z_{k},Z_{\ell})-\bar{g}_{Z,k}-\bar{g}_{Z,\ell}+\bar{g}_{Z})

(19)

for all indices
i,j,k,\ell\in[n]
. With
V(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})
as its test statistic, the standard wild bootstrap test only depends on the data via the
n^{2}/2
sufficient statistics

\textstyle\mathcal{S}_{1}\triangleq\big\{

\textstyle\sum_{a,b\in\{i,\texttt{swap}(i)\}}(G_{ab,ba},\,G_{ab,\texttt{swap}(b)\texttt{swap}(a)})\mid i\in[n/2]\big\}\text{ and }

(20)

\textstyle\mathcal{S}_{2}\triangleq\big\{

\textstyle\sum_{a\in\{i,\texttt{swap}(i)\},b\in\{j,\texttt{swap}(j)\}}(G_{ab,ba},\,G_{ab,\texttt{swap}(b)\texttt{swap}(a)}),

(21)

\textstyle\sum_{a\in\{i,\texttt{swap}(i)\},b\in\{j,\texttt{swap}(j)\}}(G_{ab,b\texttt{swap}(a)},\,G_{ab,\texttt{swap}(b)a})\mid j<i\in[n/2]\big\}.

(22)

since, for each wild bootstrap permutation
\pi
and
j\in[n]
, we have
\pi(j)\in\{j,\texttt{swap}(j)\}
and

\textstyle 4V(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z}^{\pi})

\textstyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}\sum_{a,b\in\{i,\texttt{swap}(i)\}}G_{ab,\pi(a)\pi(b)}

(23)

\textstyle+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\sum_{a\in\{i,\texttt{swap}(i)\},b\in\{j,\texttt{swap}(j)\}}G_{ab,\pi(a)\pi(b)}.

(24)

Hence, if we let
c_{g}
denote the maximum time required to evaluate
g_{Y}
or
g_{Z}
,
then one can precompute and store the elements of
\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{2}
given
\Theta(c_{g}n^{2})
time and
\Theta(n^{2})
memory and, thereafter, compute all permuted test statistics using
\Theta(Bn^{2})
elementary operations.
When the quadratic memory cost is unsupportable, the elements of
\mathcal{S}_{2}
can instead be recomputed for each permutation at a total cost of
\Theta(c_{g}Bn^{2})
time and
\Theta(n)
memory.

3.4
Cheap wild bootstrap tests of independence

To alleviate both the time and memory burden of standard wild bootstrap testing, we again propose to permute datapoint bins instead of individual points.
To simplify our presentation, we will assume that
s
is even and divides
n
evenly.
As before, we partition the indices
[n]
into
s
consecutive sets
\mathcal{I}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{I}_{s}
of equal size,
identify the corresponding datapoint bins
\mathbb{Z}_{j}\triangleq(Z_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}
, and define the bin-permuted sample
\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}\triangleq(\mathbb{Z}_{\pi(i)})_{i=1}^{s}
for each wild bootstrap permutation
\pi
on
[s]
.
If we further define the combination bins
\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime}\triangleq\mathcal{I}_{i}\cup\mathcal{I}_{i+s/2}
for each
i\in[\frac{s}{2}]
, then
the bin-wild-bootstrapped V-statistic

\textstyle 4V(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z}^{\pi})

\textstyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{s/2}D_{i\pi(i)}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{s/2}\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}S_{ij\pi(j)\pi(i)}

(25)

only depends on the data via the
s^{2}/2
sufficient statistics

\textstyle\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\prime}\triangleq\big\{

\textstyle(D_{ii},\,D_{i(i+s/2)})\triangleq\sum_{a,b\in\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime}}(G_{ab,ba},\,G_{ab,\texttt{swap}(b)\texttt{swap}(a)})\mid i\in[\frac{s}{2}]\big\}\text{ and }

(26)

\textstyle\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime}\triangleq\big\{

\textstyle(S_{ijji},\,S_{ij(j+s/2)(i+s/2)})\triangleq\sum_{a\in\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime},\,b\in\mathcal{I}_{j}^{\prime}}(G_{ab,ba},\,G_{ab,\texttt{swap}(b)\texttt{swap}(a)}),

(27)

\textstyle(S_{ijj(i+s/2)},\,S_{ij(j+s/2)i})\triangleq\sum_{a\in\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime},\,b\in\mathcal{I}_{j}^{\prime}}(G_{ab,b\texttt{swap}(a)},\,G_{ab,\texttt{swap}(b)a})\mid j<i\in[\frac{s}{2}]\big\}.

(28)

Hence, one can precompute and store the elements of
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime}
using only
\Theta(s^{2})
memory and
\Theta(c_{g}n^{2})
time and subsequently compute all bin-permuted statistics using only
\Theta(Bs^{2})
elementary operations.

Alg.

2

details the steps of this
cheap independence test
which recovers the standard wild bootstrap permutation test when
s=n
.

As in the homogeneity setting, we find that the overhead of cheap permutation is negligible whenever
Bs^{2}\ll c_{\phi}n^{2}
, and we will see in
Secs.

4
and
6
that a slow-growing or constant setting of
s
suffices to maintain power while substantially reducing runtime.

A natural alternative to using wild bootstrap permutations is to instead employ uniformly random permutations
\pi
on
[s]

[
62
, see, e.g.,]
. Unfortunately, while
s^{2}/2
scalar statistics suffice to compute any bin-wild-bootstrapped V-statistic, at least
\Theta(s^{4})
statistics of the form

\textstyle\sum_{a\in\mathcal{I}_{i}^{\prime},\,b\in\mathcal{I}_{j}^{\prime},c\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\prime},d\in\mathcal{I}_{l}^{\prime}}G_{ab,cd}\quad\text{for}\quad i,j,k,l\in[s]

(29)

are required to compute all bin-permuted V-statistics,
4V(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z}^{\pi})
.
As a result, the cheap wild bootstrap test of independence enjoys substantial speed and memory benefits over the analogous cheap (uniform) permutation test of independence.
In
Secs.

5
and
4
, we will also establish power guarantees for cheap wild bootstrapping that match the best known rates for standard permutation testing and the minimax optimal rates for independence testing. We therefore recommend the cheap wild bootstrap independence test of
Alg.

2
as a fast and powerful replacement for both wild boostrap and uniform permutation tests.

Algorithm 2

Cheap independence testing

Input:

Paired sample
{((Y_{i},Z_{i}))}_{i=1}^{n}
,
bin count
s
, base functions
g_{Y}
,
g_{Z}
(
Def.

3
), level
\alpha
, wild bootstrap count
B

1

Split the indices
\{1,\ldots,n\}
into
s
consecutive bins
\mathcal{I}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{I}_{s}
of equal size

2

// Compute sufficient statistics using
\Theta(c_{g}n^{2})
time and
\Theta(s^{2})
memory

3

for

a\in[n]

do

(\bar{g}_{Y,a},\bar{g}_{Z,a})\leftarrow\frac{1}{n}\sum_{b=1}^{n}(g_{Y}(Y_{a},Y_{b}),g_{Z}(Z_{a},Z_{b}))
;

4

(\bar{g}_{Y},\bar{g}_{Z})\leftarrow\frac{1}{n}\!\sum_{a=1}^{n}(\bar{g}_{Y,a},\bar{g}_{Z,a})

5

Define the mappings

\texttt{swap}(a)\triangleq a+\frac{n}{2}-n\mathbf{1}_{a>\frac{n}{2}}

and
G_{ab,cd}\triangleq(g_{Y}(Y_{a},Y_{b})-\bar{g}_{Y,a}-\bar{g}_{Y,b}+\bar{g}_{Y})(g_{Z}(Z_{c},Z_{d})-\bar{g}_{Z,c}-\bar{g}_{Z,d}+\bar{g}_{Z})

6

for

i\in[s]

do

7

(D_{ii},\,D_{i(i+s/2)})\leftarrow\sum_{a,b\in\mathcal{I}_{i}}(G_{ab,ba},\,G_{ab,\texttt{swap}(b)\texttt{swap}(a)})

8

for

j\in[i-1]

do

9

(S_{ijji},\,S_{ij(j+s/2)(i+s/2)})\leftarrow\sum_{a\in\mathcal{I}_{i},b\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}(G_{ab,ba},\,G_{ab,\texttt{swap}(b)\texttt{swap}(a)})

10

(S_{ij(j+s/2)i},\,S_{ijj(i+s/2)})\leftarrow\sum_{a\in\mathcal{I}_{i},b\in\mathcal{I}_{j}}(G_{ab,\texttt{swap}(b)a},\,G_{ab,b\texttt{swap}(a)})

11

end for

12

end for

13

// Compute original and permuted test statistics using
\Theta(Bs^{2})
elementary operations

14

for

b=0,1,2,\ldots,B

do

15

\pi\leftarrow
identity permutation if
b=0
else wild bootstrap permutation of
[s]

16

T_{b}\leftarrow\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{s}D_{i\pi(i)}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}S_{ij\pi(j)\pi(i)}

17

end for

18

// Return rejection probability

19

R\leftarrow 1+
number of permuted statistics
(T_{b})_{b=1}^{B}

smaller than
T_{0}
if ties are broken at random

20

return

\Delta(\mathbb{X})\triangleq\min(1,\max(0,R\!-\!(1\!-\!\alpha)(B\!+\!1)))

3.5
Finite-sample exactness

We conclude this section by noting that the cheap permutation tests of
Algs.

1
and
2
are
exact
, that is, their sizes exactly match the nominal level
\alpha
for all sample sizes, data distributions, and permutation counts
B
.
This is a principal advantage of permutation testing over asymptotic tests that (a) require precise knowledge of a test statistic’s asymptotic null distribution and (b) can violate the level constraint at any given sample size.

Proposition 1

(Cheap permutation tests are exact)
.

Under their respective null hypotheses, the cheap permutation tests of
Algs.

1
and
2
reject the null with probability
\alpha
.

Proof.

Since cheap permutation tests are standard permutation tests over bins of datapoints and
Algs.

1
and
2
both employ the randomized rejection rule of
Hoeffding [28]
, exactness follows immediately from Prop. 3 of
Hemerik and Goeman [27]
.
∎

4
Power of cheap testing

We now turn our attention to the power of cheap testing with homogeneity U-statistics (
Def.

2
) and independence V-statistics (
Def.

3
).
All of our results apply to any choice of permutation count
B
and are non-vacuous exactly when
B\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}-1
.

4.1
Power guarantees for homogeneity testing

For the homogeneity U-statistic of
Def.

2
, define the symmetrized version of

h_{\mathrm{ho}}
,

\textstyle\overline{h}_{\mathrm{ho}}(y_{1},y_{2};z_{1},z_{2})\triangleq\frac{1}{2!2!}\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{2}}\sum_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbf{i}_{2}^{2}}h_{\mathrm{ho}}(y_{i_{1}},y_{i_{2}};z_{j_{1}},z_{j_{2}}),

(30)

and the
variance components

\textstyle\psi_{Y,1}

\textstyle\triangleq\max\{\mathrm{Var}(\mathbb{E}[\overline{h}_{\mathrm{ho}}(Y_{1},Y_{2};Z_{1},Z_{2})|Y_{1}]),\mathrm{Var}(\mathbb{E}[\overline{h}_{\mathrm{ho}}(Z_{3},Y_{2};Z_{1},Z_{2})|Z_{3}])\},

(31)

\textstyle\psi_{Z,1}

\textstyle\triangleq\max\{\mathrm{Var}(\mathbb{E}[\overline{h}_{\mathrm{ho}}(Y_{1},Y_{2};Z_{1},Z_{2})|Z_{1}]),\mathrm{Var}(\mathbb{E}[\overline{h}_{\mathrm{ho}}(Y_{1},Y_{2};Y_{3},Z_{2})|Y_{3}])\},\text{ and }

(32)

\textstyle\psi_{YZ,2}

\textstyle\triangleq\max\{\mathbb{E}[g^{2}(Y_{1},Y_{2})],\ \mathbb{E}[g^{2}(Y_{1},Z_{1})],\ \mathbb{E}[g^{2}(Z_{1},Z_{2})]\}.

(33)

Our first theorem, proved in
App.

B
, bounds the minimum separation between
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
required for standard and cheap homogeneity tests to reject with power at least
1-\beta
.
Here, separation is measured by the mean of the test statistic
\mathbb{E}[U_{n_{1},n_{2}}]
, which is zero under the null (
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{Q}
) but may be non-zero under the alternative (
\mathbb{P}\neq\mathbb{Q}
).

Theorem 1

(Power of cheap homogeneity: finite variance)
.

Consider a homogeneity U-statistic
U_{n_{1},n_{2}}
(
Def.

2
) with finite variance components
32
, sample sizes
n_{2}\geq n_{1}\geq 4
, and
n\triangleq n_{1}+n_{2}
.
A cheap homogeneity test (
Alg.

1
with
s\geq 4
) based on
U_{n_{1},n_{2}}
has power at least
1-\beta
whenever

\textstyle\mathbb{E}[U_{n_{1},n_{2}}]

\textstyle\geq\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2},s}\triangleq\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2}}+\mathbf{1}_{s<n}\frac{\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2}}+\sqrt{\frac{201s^{2}}{4n^{2}}\psi_{YZ,2}+{\frac{12s}{n}\max(\psi_{Y,1},\psi_{Z,1})}{}}}{\sqrt{s^{3}\frac{\beta\alpha^{\star}}{24(1-\alpha^{\star})}\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}}-1}

(34)

\textstyle\quad\text{for}\quad\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2}}

\textstyle\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{3-\beta}{\beta}\big(\frac{4\psi_{Y,1}}{n_{1}}\!+\!\frac{4\psi_{Z,1}}{n_{2}}\big)}+\sqrt{\frac{1-\alpha^{\star}}{\alpha^{\star}}\frac{(36n_{1}^{2}+36n_{2}^{2}+198n_{1}n_{2})\psi_{YZ,2}}{\beta n_{1}(n_{1}-1)n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}},

(35)

\alpha^{\star}\triangleq\frac{\alpha}{2e}\big(\frac{\beta}{3}\big)^{1/\lfloor{\alpha(B+1)}\rfloor}
,
and

\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}\triangleq\frac{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)^{2}n_{2}(n_{2}-1)^{2}}{n^{6}}
.

Remark 1

(Properties of
\alpha^{\star}
)
.

The parameter
\alpha^{\star}
lies in the range
[\frac{\alpha}{2e}\frac{\beta}{3},\frac{\alpha}{2e})
for all
B\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}-1
with
\alpha^{\star}\geq\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2e}
for
B\geq\frac{\log(3/\beta)}{\alpha\log(1/\alpha)}-1

and

\alpha^{\star}\geq\frac{\alpha}{2e^{2}}
for
B\geq\frac{\log(3/\beta)}{\alpha}-1
.

Remark 2

(Equivalent separation rates and thresholds)
.

We have
\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2},s}=\Theta(\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2}})
whenever
s\geq\sqrt[3]{\frac{24(1-\alpha^{\star})(1+\Omega(1))}{\beta\alpha^{\star}\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}}}

and
\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2},s}=\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2}}(1+o(1))
whenever

s=\omega(\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}})
.

The separation threshold
\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2}}
for standard permutation testing is a refined, quantitative version of the one derived in
[
31
, Thm. 4.1]
. Here, we keep track of all numerical factors and, thanks to the quantile comparison method introduced in
Sec.

4.3
,
establish a tighter dependency on the number of permutations
B
.
The separation threshold
\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2},s}
for cheap permutation testing is entirely new and implies two remarkable properties summarized in
Rem.

2
.
First, the cheap and standard thresholds are
asymptotically equivalent
(in the strong sense that
\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2},s}/\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2}}=1+o(1)
) whenever
s=\omega(\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}})
.
For
n_{1}=\Theta(n_{2})
, this occurs whenever
s
grows unboundedly with
n
, even if the growth rate is arbitrarily slow.
Second, the cheap and standard
separation rates
are identical (i.e.,
\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2},s}=\Theta(\gamma_{n_{1},n_{2}})
) whenever
s\geq\sqrt[3]{\frac{24(1-\alpha^{\star})(1+\Omega(1))}{\beta\alpha^{\star}\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}}}
. Hence, when
n_{1}=\Theta(n_{2})
, cheap testing can match the separation rate of standard permutation testing using a
constant
number of bins independent of the sample size
n
.

4.2
Power guarantees for independence testing

For the independence V-statistic of
Def.

3
,
we introduce the shorthand
X_{i}\triangleq(Y_{i},Z_{i})
and
define a symmetrized version of

h_{\mathrm{in}}
,

\textstyle\overline{h}_{\mathrm{in}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})

\textstyle\triangleq\frac{1}{4!}\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\mathbf{i}_{4}^{4}}h_{\mathrm{in}}(x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{2}},x_{i_{3}},x_{i_{4}}),

(36)

the
variance components

\textstyle\begin{split}\textstyle\psi_{1}^{\prime}&\textstyle\triangleq\mathrm{Var}(\mathbb{E}[\overline{h}_{\mathrm{in}}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})\mid X_{1}]),\\
\textstyle\quad\tilde{\psi}_{1}^{\prime}&\textstyle\triangleq\mathrm{Var}(\mathbb{E}[\overline{h}_{\mathrm{in}}((Y_{1},Z_{\pi_{1}}),(Y_{2},Z_{\pi_{2}}),(Y_{3},Z_{\pi_{3}}),(Y_{4},Z_{\pi_{4}}))\mid(Y_{1},Z_{\pi_{1}},\pi_{1})]),\quad\text{and}\\
\textstyle\psi_{2}^{\prime}&\textstyle\triangleq 16\max\big\{\mathbb{E}[g_{Y}^{2}(Y_{1},Y_{2})g_{Z}^{2}(Z_{i},Z_{j})]:(i,j)\in\{(1,2),(1,3),(3,4)\}\big\},\end{split}

where
\pi
is an independent wild bootstrap permutation of
[n]
, and the
mean component

\textstyle\tilde{\xi}

\textstyle\triangleq\max\{|\mathbb{E}[h_{\mathrm{in}}((Y_{i_{1}},Z_{i^{\prime}_{1}}),(Y_{i_{2}},Z_{i^{\prime}_{2}}),(Y_{i_{3}},Z_{i^{\prime}_{3}}),(Y_{i_{4}},Z_{i^{\prime}_{4}}))]|:i_{k},i^{\prime}_{k}\in[8],\forall k\in[4]\}.

(37)

Our next theorem, proved in
App.

E
, bounds the minimum separation between
\Pi
and
\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q}
required for standard and cheap independence tests to reject with power at least
1-\beta
.
Here, separation is measured by

\textstyle\mathcal{U}

\textstyle\triangleq\mathbb{E}[h_{\mathrm{in}}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})],

(38)

the expected U-statistic corresponding to the V-statistic
V_{n}
.
Notably,
\mathcal{U}
is zero under the null (
\Pi=\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q}
) but may be non-zero under the alternative (
\Pi\neq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q}
).

Theorem 2

(Power of cheap independence: finite variance)
.

Consider an independence V-statistic
V_{n}
(
Def.

3
) with finite variance components
Sec.

4.2
,

\alpha^{\star}\triangleq\frac{\alpha}{2e}\big(\frac{\beta}{3}\big)^{1/\lfloor{\alpha(B+1)}\rfloor}
,
and
n\geq 8
.
A cheap independence test (
Alg.

2
) based on
V_{n}
has power at least
1-\beta
whenever

\textstyle\mathcal{U}

\textstyle\geq\gamma_{n,s}\triangleq\gamma_{n}+\mathbf{1}_{s<n}\frac{\gamma_{n}(s+1)+4s^{2}\sqrt{\big(\frac{12}{\alpha^{\star}\beta}-2\big)\big(\tilde{\psi}_{1}^{\prime}\big(\frac{2304}{ns}+\frac{460800}{ns^{2}}\big)+\psi_{2}^{\prime}\frac{4147200}{n^{2}s}\big)}}{3s^{2}-s-1}\!\!

(39)

\textstyle\text{for}\ \gamma_{n}

\textstyle\triangleq\frac{4}{3}\big(\sqrt{(\frac{3}{\beta}\!-\!1)\big(\frac{16\psi_{1}^{\prime}}{n}\!+\!\frac{144\psi_{2}^{\prime}}{n^{2}}\big)}\!+\!\sqrt{\big(\frac{12}{\alpha^{\star}\beta}\!-\!2\big)\big(\frac{32\tilde{\psi}_{1}^{\prime}}{n}\!+\!\frac{960\psi_{2}^{\prime}}{n^{2}}\big)}\!+\!\frac{16}{n}\tilde{\xi}\!+\!\frac{24}{n}\sqrt{\psi_{2}^{\prime}}\big).

(40)

Remark 3

(Equivalent separation rates and thresholds)
.

We have
\gamma_{n,s}=\Theta(\gamma_{n})
for
any
choice of
s

and
\gamma_{n,s}=\gamma_{n}(1+o(1))
whenever
s=\omega(1)
.

The separation threshold
\gamma_{n}
is a quantitative analogue of the threshold derived in
[
31
, Thm. 5.1]
. The main structural difference is that
Thm.

2
analyzes the wild-bootstrapped V-statistic
V_{n}
while Thm. 5.1 of
[
31
]
analyzes a permutation test built on the corresponding independence U-statistic; this accounts for the two quantities in
\gamma_{n}
that have no counterpart in
[
31
, Thm. 5.1]
. The term
\tilde{\xi}

37
collects the diagonal contributions retained by the V-statistic but discarded by the U-statistic and enters only at the lower order
O(1/n)
. The term
\tilde{\psi}_{1}^{\prime}

Sec.

4.2
is the first-order variance component measured under the wild-bootstrap resampling distribution rather than under
\Pi
, entering at the same leading order
O(1/\sqrt{n})
as
\psi_{1}^{\prime}
. We will see in
Lem.

16
that
\tilde{\psi}_{1}^{\prime}
is bounded by a constant multiple of
\psi_{1}^{\prime}
for positive-definite kernels so that
\gamma_{n}
matches the separation rate of
[
31
, Thm. 5.1]
up to constant factors for such kernels.
Thm.

2
additionally keeps track of all numerical constants and, thanks to the quantile comparison method of
Sec.

4.3
, yields a tighter dependency on the number of permutations
B
.

The separation threshold
\gamma_{n,s}
for cheap permutation testing is entirely new and satisfies two remarkable properties (
Rem.

3
). First, the cheap and standard thresholds are again asymptotically equivalent whenever
s=\omega(1)
. Second, the cheap and standard separation
rates
are identical (i.e.,
\gamma_{n,s}=\Theta(\gamma_{n})
) for any choice of
s
, even
s=2
. Hence, cheap independence testing can match the separation rate of standard testing using a
constant
number of bins independent of the sample size
n
.

4.3
Quantile comparison method

At the heart of our power guarantees is a quantile comparison method (
Proposition

2
) suitable for bounding the power of Monte Carlo permutation tests. Related results have appeared in the literature under the name
two moments method
, as they control quantiles using the first two moments of the test statistic.
This approach to assessing non-asymptotic power was pioneered by
Fromont, Laurent and Reynaud-Bouret [21]
in the context of homogeneity testing and later developed by
Kim, Balakrishnan and Wasserman [31]
,
Schrab et al. [52]
,
Domingo-Enrich, Dwivedi and Mackey [16]
,
Kim and Schrab [32]
, among others.
Earlier guarantees due to
[
31
,
52
,
32
]
placed undue constraints on the number of permutations
B
. For example, Prop. D.1 of
[
31
]
requires
B\geq\frac{8}{\alpha^{2}}\log(\frac{4}{\beta})
, Thm. 5 of
[
52
]
requires
B\geq\frac{3}{\alpha^{2}}(\log(\frac{8}{\beta})+\alpha(1-\alpha))
, and Lem. 21 of
[
32
]
requires
B\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}(\log(\frac{1}{\beta})+1-\alpha)
.
Here we adapt a tighter argument developed for homogeneity testing with a bounded positive-definite kernel
[
16
, Lem. 6]
and extend it to all forms of permutation test and, more generally, to any test that thresholds using the quantiles of i.i.d. perturbations.
The result, proved in
App.

H
,
provides non-vacuous power guarantees for all permutation counts
B\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}-1
.

Proposition 2

(Quantile comparison method)
.

Consider a test statistic
T(\mathbb{X})
and an auxiliary sequence
(T_{b})_{b=1}^{B}
generated i.i.d. and independently of
T(\mathbb{X})
given
\mathbb{X}
.
Suppose that a test
\Delta(\mathbb{X})
rejects whenever
T(\mathbb{X})
exceeds the
b_{\alpha}=\lceil{(1-\alpha)(B+1)}\rceil
smallest values in
(T_{b})_{b=1}^{B}
.
Then
\mathbb{E}[\Delta(\mathbb{X})]\geq 1-\beta
if any population parameter
\mathcal{T}
satisfies

\textstyle\mathcal{T}\geq\Psi_{n}(\alpha^{\star},\frac{\beta}{3})+\Phi_{n}(\frac{\beta}{3})\quad\text{for}\quad\alpha^{\star}\triangleq\frac{\alpha}{2e}\big(\frac{\beta}{3}\big)^{1/\lfloor{\alpha(B+1)}\rfloor},

(41)

where
(\Phi_{n},\Psi_{\mathbb{X}},\Psi_{n})
are complementary quantile bounds satisfying

\textstyle\Pr(\mathcal{T}-T(\mathbb{X})\geq\Phi_{n}(\frac{\beta}{3}))\leq\frac{\beta}{3},

(42)

\textstyle\Pr(T_{1}\geq\Psi_{\mathbb{X}}(\alpha^{\star})\mid\mathbb{X})\leq\alpha^{\star}\quad\text{almost surely,}\quad\text{ and }

(43)

\textstyle\Pr(\Psi_{\mathbb{X}}(\alpha^{\star})\geq\Psi_{n}(\alpha^{\star},\frac{\beta}{3}))\leq\frac{\beta}{3}.

(44)

When the test statistic
T(\mathbb{X})
has finite variance and the auxiliary variable
T_{1}
has almost-surely finite conditional variance, Cantelli’s inequality yields the following corollary, a refinement of the two moments method of
[
31
, Prop. D.1]
.
See
App.

I
for the proof.

Corollary 1

(Refined two moments method)
.

If
\mathrm{Var}(T(\mathbb{X})),\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(T_{1}\mid\mathbb{X})]<\infty
, then the following functions satisfy the requirements
42
,
43
, and
44
of
Proposition

2
for
\mathcal{T}=\mathbb{E}[T(\mathbb{X})]
:

\textstyle\Phi_{n}(\beta)

\textstyle=\sqrt{(\frac{1}{\beta}-1)\mathrm{Var}(T(\mathbb{X}))},\quad\Psi_{\mathbb{X}}(\alpha)=\sqrt{(\frac{1}{\alpha}-1)\mathrm{Var}(T_{1}\mid\mathbb{X})}+\mathbb{E}[T_{1}\mid\mathbb{X}],

(45)

\textstyle\Psi_{n}(\alpha,\beta)

\textstyle=\sqrt{(\frac{1}{\alpha\beta}\!-\!\frac{1}{\beta})\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(T_{1}\mid\mathbb{X})]}\quad\text{when}\quad\mathbb{E}[T_{1}\mid\mathbb{X}]=0\quad\text{almost surely,}\quad\text{and}

(46)

\textstyle\Psi_{n}(\alpha,\beta)

\textstyle=\sqrt{(\frac{2}{\alpha\beta}\!-\!\frac{2}{\beta})\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(T_{1}\mid\mathbb{X})]}+\mathbb{E}[T_{1}]+\!\sqrt{\!(\frac{2}{\beta}-1)\mathrm{Var}(\mathbb{E}[T_{1}\mid\mathbb{X}])}\ \text{otherwise.}

(47)

5
Minimax optimality of cheap testing

In this section, we apply the power results of
Sec.

4
to establish the minimax rate optimality of cheap testing in a variety of settings.
Below, for discrete and absolutely continuous signed measures
\mu
, we make use of the standard
L^{2}
norm on densities,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mu}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\triangleq\int(\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}(x))^{2}d\nu(x)
, where
\nu
is the counting measure if
\mu
is supported on a discrete set
\mathcal{X}
and
\nu
is the Lebesgue measure when
\mu
has a Lebesgue density.
Note that

\textstyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbb{P}-\mathbb{Q}}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\begin{cases}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}(p(x)-q(x))^{2}&\text{if }\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\text{ have probability mass functions $p,q$ on a set $\mathcal{X}$ }\\
\int(p(x)-q(x))^{2}dx&\text{if }\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\text{ have Lebesgue densities $p,q$. }\end{cases}

(48)

We also define the Hölder-space ball,

\textstyle H^{d}_{\tau}(L)\triangleq\big\{f:[0,1]^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\,\big|\,

\textstyle|f^{(\lfloor{{\tau}}\rfloor)}(x)-f^{(\lfloor{{\tau}}\rfloor)}(x^{\prime})|\leq L\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x-x^{\prime}}}}\right\|_{2}^{{\tau}-\lfloor{{\tau}}\rfloor},\quad\forall x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1]^{d},

(49)

\textstyle|f^{({\tau}^{\prime})}(x)|\leq L,\quad\forall x\in[0,1]^{d},\quad{\tau}^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,\lfloor{\tau}\rfloor\}\big\}.

(50)

5.1
Discrete homogeneity testing

Our first result, proved in
Sec.

J.1
, shows that cheap homogeneity testing is minimax rate optimal for detecting discrete
L^{2}
separations.

Proposition 3

(Power of cheap testing: discrete
L^{2}
homogeneity)
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are discrete distributions on a finite set
\mathcal{X}

with
b_{(1)}\triangleq\max(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbb{P}}}}\right\|_{2}^{2},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbb{Q}}}}\right\|_{2}^{2})
. A cheap homogeneity test (
Alg.

1
) based on a homogeneity U-statistic (
Def.

2
) with
n_{1}\leq n_{2}
and base function
g(y,z)=\mathbf{1}_{y=z}

has power at least
1-\beta
whenever

\textstyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbb{P}-\mathbb{Q}}}}\right\|_{2}

\textstyle\geq\frac{Cb_{(1)}^{1/4}}{\sqrt[6]{\beta\alpha^{\star}}\sqrt{\min(\beta,\alpha^{\star})\,n_{1}}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{s^{3}\frac{\beta\alpha^{\star}}{24(1-\alpha^{\star})}\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}}-1}\bigg)

(51)

for a universal constant
C
,
\alpha^{\star}\triangleq\frac{\alpha}{2e}\big(\frac{\beta}{3}\big)^{1/\lfloor{\alpha(B+1)}\rfloor}
,
and

\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}\triangleq\frac{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)^{2}n_{2}(n_{2}-1)^{2}}{n^{6}}
.

Remark 4

(Cheap optimality: discrete
L^{2}
homogeneity)
.

The cheap test of
Proposition

3
achieves the minimax optimal separation rate of order
b_{(1)}^{1/4}/\sqrt{n_{1}}

[
31
, Prop. 4.4]

whenever

s\geq\sqrt[3]{\frac{24(1-\alpha^{\star})(1+\Omega(1))}{\beta\alpha^{\star}\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}}}
.

5.2
Hölder homogeneity testing

Our second result, proved in
Sec.

J.2
, shows that cheap homogeneity testing is minimax rate optimal for detecting Hölder
L^{2}
separations.
The test statistic, based on discretization of the unit cube, is the same used in
[
31
, Prop. 4.6]
.

Proposition 4

(Power of cheap testing: Hölder
L^{2}
homogeneity)
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are distributions on
[0,1]^{d}
with Lebesgue densities belonging to the Hölder ball
H^{d}_{\tau}(L)

50
.
Let
\{B_{1},\dots,B_{K}\}
be a partition of
[0,1]^{d}
into
d
-dimensional hypercubes with side length
1/\lfloor{n_{1}^{2/(4\tau+d)}}\rfloor
.
A cheap homogeneity test (
Alg.

1
) based on a homogeneity U-statistic (
Def.

2
) with
n_{1}\leq n_{2}
and base function
g(y,z)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{1}_{y,z\in B_{k}}

has power at least
1-\beta
whenever

\textstyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbb{P}-\mathbb{Q}}}}\right\|_{2}

\textstyle\geq(\frac{1}{n_{1}})^{2\tau/(4\tau+d)}\,\frac{C\sqrt[4]{L}}{\sqrt[6]{\beta\alpha^{\star}}\sqrt{\min(\beta,\alpha^{\star})}}\,\bigg(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{s^{3}\frac{\beta\alpha^{\star}}{24(1-\alpha^{\star})}\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}}-1}\bigg)

(52)

for a universal constant
C
,
\alpha^{\star}\triangleq\frac{\alpha}{2e}\big(\frac{\beta}{3}\big)^{1/\lfloor{\alpha(B+1)}\rfloor}
, and

\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}\triangleq\frac{n_{1}(n_{1}-1)^{2}n_{2}(n_{2}-1)^{2}}{n^{6}}
.

Remark 5

(Cheap optimality: Hölder
L^{2}
homogeneity)
.

The cheap test of
Proposition

4
achieves the minimax optimal separation rate of order
n_{1}^{-2\tau/(4\tau+d)}

[
2
, Thm. 3]

whenever

s\geq\sqrt[3]{\frac{24(1-\alpha^{\star})(1+\Omega(1))}{\beta\alpha^{\star}\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}}}
.

5.3
MMD homogeneity testing

Recently,
Kim and Schrab [33, Thm. 7]
established a lower-bound of order
n_{1}^{-1/2}
on the minimax-optimal detectable separation in maximum mean discrepancy (
\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
,
8
) when
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the positive-definite kernel
\mathbf{k}
is bounded, translation-invariant (i.e.,
\mathbf{k}(x,y)=\kappa(x-y)
for some
\kappa
), and non-constant.
To show that cheap homogeneity testing can recover this optimal rate, we first establish two refined guarantees for testing with positive-definite kernels.

When the base function
g
is a positive-definite kernel
\mathbf{k}
,
the expected homogeneity U-statistic (
Def.

2
) is nonnegative and equal to the squared MMD. In this case,
Thm.

1
also yields a detectable separation threshold of order
\sqrt{1/n_{1}+1/n_{2}}
for the root mean test statistic,
\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[U_{n_{1},n_{2}}]}
.

Corollary 2

(Power of cheap homogeneity: finite variance, PD)
.

Under the assumptions of
Thm.

1
and notation of
Example

1
, suppose that
g
is a positive-definite kernel
\mathbf{k}
with

\textstyle\xi_{\mathbb{Q}},\,\xi_{\mathbb{P}}<\infty\quad\text{for}\quad\xi_{\mu}\triangleq\max(\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}(\delta_{Y_{1}},\mu)],\,\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}(\delta_{Z_{1}},\mu)]).

(53)

A cheap homogeneity test based on
U_{n_{1},n_{2}}
has power at least
1-\beta
whenever

\textstyle\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})

\textstyle\geq\epsilon_{n_{1},n_{2},s}\triangleq\epsilon_{n_{1},n_{2}}+\mathbf{1}_{s<n}\frac{\epsilon_{n_{1},n_{2}}+\sqrt{\frac{12s}{n}\max(\xi_{\mathbb{Q}},\xi_{\mathbb{P}})}+\sqrt[4]{\frac{201\beta\alpha^{\star}s^{5}\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}}{96(1-\alpha^{\star})n^{2}}\psi_{YZ,2}}}{\sqrt{s^{3}\frac{\beta\alpha^{\star}}{24(1-\alpha^{\star})}\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}}-1}

(54)

\textstyle\quad\text{for}\quad\epsilon_{n_{1},n_{2}}

\textstyle\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{3-\beta}{\beta}\big(\frac{4\xi_{\mathbb{Q}}}{n_{1}}\!+\!\frac{4\xi_{\mathbb{P}}}{n_{2}}\big)}+\sqrt[4]{\frac{1-\alpha^{\star}}{\alpha^{\star}}\frac{(36n_{1}^{2}+36n_{2}^{2}+198n_{1}n_{2})\psi_{YZ,2}}{\beta n_{1}(n_{1}-1)n_{2}(n_{2}-1)}}.

(55)

Remark 6

(Equivalent separation rates and thresholds)
.

We have
\epsilon_{n_{1},n_{2},s}=\Theta(\epsilon_{n_{1},n_{2}})
whenever
s\geq\sqrt[3]{\frac{24(1-\alpha^{\star})(1+\Omega(1))}{\beta\alpha^{\star}\rho_{n_{1}n_{2}}}}

and
\epsilon_{n_{1},n_{2},s}=\epsilon_{n_{1},n_{2}}(1+o(1))
whenever
s=\omega(\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}})
.

The proof of
Corollary

2
can be found in
App.

C
.
Our next result establishes improved power guarantees for cheap testing with only logarithmic dependence on
\beta
and
\alpha^{\star}
under a commonly-satisfied sub-Gaussianity assumption.

Theorem 3

(Power of cheap homogeneity: sub-Gaussian, PD)
.

Under the assumptions of
Thm.

1
and notation of
Example

1
, suppose that
g
is a positive-definite kernel
\mathbf{k}
with finite sub-Gaussian parameters

\textstyle\sigma_{\mathbb{P}},\ \sigma_{\mathbb{Q}}<\infty\quad\text{for}\quad\sigma_{\mu}\triangleq\sup_{p\geq 1}\frac{(\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}|\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{X}\sim\mu}\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}(\delta_{X},\delta_{\tilde{X}})|^{p})^{1/p}}{\sqrt{p}}

(56)

and MMD moments

\textstyle\mathrm{M}_{4,\mathbb{P}},\ \mathrm{M}_{4,\mathbb{Q}}<\infty\quad\text{for}\quad\mathrm{M}_{p,\mu}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}^{p}(\delta_{X},\mu).

(57)

Suppose moreover that
s_{1}\triangleq\frac{sn_{1}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}>2\log(4/\alpha^{\star})+1

for
\alpha^{\star}\triangleq\frac{\alpha}{2e}\big(\frac{\beta}{3}\big)^{1/\lfloor{\alpha(B+1)}\rfloor}
.
A cheap homogeneity test based on
U_{n_{1},n_{2}}
has power at least
1-\beta
whenever

\textstyle\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\geq\frac{1}{1-\frac{2\log(4/\alpha^{\star})+1}{s_{1}}}\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{40\sqrt{2\log(\frac{2}{\alpha^{\star}})/3}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4}+\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(\frac{\beta}{9})^{2})+4(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}^{2}+\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(\frac{\beta}{9})^{2})}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}+\frac{3\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{P}}(\frac{\beta}{3})}{\sqrt{n_{1}-1}}+\frac{3\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\frac{\beta}{3})}{\sqrt{n_{2}-1}}

(58)

\textstyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\frac{40\sqrt{\log(\frac{2}{\alpha^{\star}})}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}+\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(\frac{\beta}{9}))}{\sqrt{3s_{1}n_{1}}}+\frac{2(\sqrt{2}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}+\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(\frac{\beta}{9}))}{s_{1}\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bigg)\quad\text{for}\quad

(59)

\textstyle\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{p}\!\triangleq\!\frac{p}{2}\sqrt{\mathrm{M}_{p,\mathbb{P}}\!+\!\mathrm{M}_{p,\mathbb{Q}}},\ \ \tilde{\mathcal{B}}(\delta)\!\triangleq\!8\sqrt{e\log(\frac{1}{\delta})(\sigma_{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\!+\!\sigma_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2})},\ \ \tilde{\Lambda}_{\mu}(\delta)\!\triangleq\!\sqrt{2\mathrm{M}_{2,\mu}}\!+\!8\sigma_{\mu}\sqrt{2e\log(\frac{1}{\delta})}.

(60)

Remark 7

(Bounded kernels are sub-Gaussian)
.

If
\mathbf{k}
is bounded, then the sub-Gaussian parameter
\sigma_{\mu}\leq 2\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sqrt{\mathbf{k}(x,x)}
for all distributions
\mu
.

Remark 8

(Cheap optimality: MMD homogeneity)
.

The cheap test of
Thm.

3
with a bounded, translation-invariant, and non-constant kernel
\mathbf{k}
achieves the minimax optimal separation rate of order
n_{1}^{-1/2}

[
33
, Thm. 7]

whenever

s=(2+\Omega(1))(2\log(4/\alpha^{\star})+1){n}/{n_{1}}
.

For balanced sample sizes (
n_{2}=\Theta(n_{1})
),
Thm.

3
, proved in
App.

D
,
establishes that cheap testing powerfully detects sub-Gaussian MMD separations of order
\frac{\sqrt{\log(1/\beta)}\sqrt[4]{\log(1/\alpha^{\star})}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}

using only
s=\Theta(\log(4/\alpha^{\star}))
bins.
This represents an exponential improvement over the order

\frac{\sqrt{1/\beta}\sqrt[4]{1/(\alpha^{\star}\beta)}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}
threshold and
s=\Omega\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt[3]{\frac{1}{\beta\alpha^{\star}}}}}\right)
bin size requirement of
Corollary

2
and recovers the minimax optimal MMD separation rate of
[
33
, Thm. 7]
with a constant bin count independent of the sample size.
In cases of extreme imbalance (
n_{2}=\omega(n_{1})
), one can choose
s=\Theta(\log(4/\alpha^{\star})n/{n_{1}})
bins for minimax optimality and still obtain a speed-up of order
n_{1}^{2}
over the
Bn^{2}
standard permutation testing overhead.

For bounded kernels bounded below by
0
,
Kim and Schrab [33, Thm. 6]
recently established a minimum separation rate upper bound of order
\frac{\sqrt{\max(\log(1/\beta),\log(1/\alpha))}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}
using a standard permutation test with
B\geq\frac{6}{\alpha}\log(2/\beta)
permutations,
n_{2}=\Theta(n_{1})
, and
\Theta(Bn^{2})
permutation overhead.
By
Thm.

3
, cheap testing in this setting can detect order
\frac{\sqrt{\log(1/\beta)}\sqrt[4]{\log(1/\alpha)}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}
separations using
B=\Omega(\frac{\log(3/\beta)}{\alpha\log(2e/\alpha)})
permutations and
s=\Theta(\log(1/\alpha))
bins. Hence, binned permutation can match both the rate and the power dependence of state-of-the-art permutation guarantees while running in time independent of
n
. By
Thm.

3
, the same guarantees also hold more generally for unbounded sub-Gaussian kernels (like distance-induced kernels
[
54
]
) and kernels that take on negative values (like the popular
\textup{sinc}(x-y)
kernel).

To interpret the sub-Gaussian bound
59
, it helps to isolate the roles of its three building blocks. The moment terms
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
aggregate the
p
-th moments
\mathrm{M}_{p,\mathbb{P}}
and
\mathrm{M}_{p,\mathbb{Q}}
of the MMD deviation
\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}(\delta_{X},\mu)
of a sample from its own distribution and thus quantify how spread out
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are in the kernel geometry. The sub-Gaussian term
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(\delta)
controls the tails of this deviation through the sub-Gaussian parameters
\sigma_{\mathbb{P}},\sigma_{\mathbb{Q}}
and is the sole source of the logarithmic confidence factors
\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}
. The term
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mu}(\delta)
combines the second moment
\mathrm{M}_{2,\mu}
with the sub-Gaussian parameter
\sigma_{\mu}
to bound the per-sample fluctuations of the statistic under
\mu
.

With this notation in hand, we can unpack the terms comprising
59
. The first three terms, of orders
n_{1}^{-1/2}
,
(n_{1}-1)^{-1/2}
, and
(n_{2}-1)^{-1/2}
, are independent of
s
and capture the irreducible cost of distinguishing the MMD signal from the sampling fluctuations of
U_{n_{1},n_{2}}
under
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
; this is the same detection cost incurred by standard permutation testing. The last two terms, of orders
(s_{1}n_{1})^{-1/2}
and
s_{1}^{-1}n_{1}^{-1/2}
, are the additional price of cheap testing with
s
bins, and both vanish as
s_{1}
grows, so that enough bins recover the standard threshold. Finally, the prefactor
(1-(2\log(4/\alpha^{\star})+1)/s_{1})^{-1}
encodes the requirement
s_{1}>2\log(4/\alpha^{\star})+1
that there be enough bins to permute and decreases to one as
s_{1}
increases.

5.4
Discrete independence testing

Our fourth result, proved in
Sec.

J.3
, shows that cheap independence testing is minimax rate optimal for detecting discrete
L^{2}
dependence.

Proposition 5

(Power of cheap testing: discrete
L^{2}
independence)
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are discrete distributions on finite sets
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
respectively with
b_{(2)}\triangleq\max(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\Pi}}}\right\|_{2}^{2},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q}}}}\right\|_{2}^{2})
, and consider the augmented samples

\textstyle\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}\triangleq((Y_{i},A_{i}))_{i=1}^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}\triangleq((Z_{i},B_{i}))_{i=1}^{n}\quad\text{for}\quad(A_{i})_{i=1}^{n},(B_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\textup{\tiny{i.i.d.}\ }}{\sim}\textnormal{Unif}((0,1)).

(61)

A cheap independence test (
Alg.

2
) based on an independence V-statistic (
Def.

3
) with paired sample
(\tilde{\mathbb{Y}},\tilde{\mathbb{Z}})
and base functions
g_{Y}((y_{1},a_{1}),(y_{2},a_{2}))=\mathbf{1}_{y_{1}=y_{2},a_{1}\neq a_{2}}
and
g_{Z}((z_{1},b_{1}),(z_{2},b_{2}))=\mathbf{1}_{z_{1}=z_{2},b_{1}\neq b_{2}}

has power at least
1-\beta
whenever

\textstyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\Pi-\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q}}}}\right\|_{2}

\textstyle\geq\frac{C\,b_{(2)}^{1/4}}{\sqrt{\beta\alpha^{\star}n}},

(62)

for a universal constant
C

and
\alpha^{\star}\triangleq\frac{\alpha}{2e}\big(\frac{\beta}{3}\big)^{1/\lfloor{\alpha(B+1)}\rfloor}
.

Remark 9

(Cheap optimality: discrete
L^{2}
independence)
.

The cheap test of
Proposition

5
achieves the minimax optimal separation rate of order
b_{(2)}^{1/4}/\sqrt{n}

[
31
, Prop. 5.4]

for
any
choice of
s
.

5.5
Hölder independence testing

Our fifth result, proved in
Sec.

J.4
, shows that cheap independence testing is minimax rate optimal for detecting Hölder
L^{2}
dependence.
The test statistic base functions, based discretization of the unit cube, are the same used in
[
31
, Prop. 5.5]
.

Proposition 6

(Power of cheap testing: Hölder
L^{2}
independence)
.

Suppose that
\Pi
is a distribution on
[0,1]^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
and that the Lebesgue densities of
\Pi
and
\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q}
belong to the Hölder ball
H^{d_{1}+d_{2}}_{\tau}(L)

50

For
a\in\{1,2\}
, let
\{B_{a,1},\dots,B_{a,K_{a}}\}
be a partition of
[0,1]^{d_{a}}
into
d_{a}
-dimensional hypercubes with side length
1/\lfloor{n^{2/(4\tau+d_{1}+d_{2})}}\rfloor
, and consider the augmented samples

\textstyle\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}\triangleq((Y_{i},A_{i}))_{i=1}^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}\triangleq((Z_{i},B_{i}))_{i=1}^{n}\quad\text{for}\quad(A_{i})_{i=1}^{n},(B_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\textup{\tiny{i.i.d.}\ }}{\sim}\textnormal{Unif}((0,1)).

(63)

A cheap independence test (
Alg.

2
) based on an independence V-statistic (
Def.

3
) with paired sample
(\tilde{\mathbb{Y}},\tilde{\mathbb{Z}})
and base functions

\textstyle g_{Y}((y_{1},a_{1}),(y_{2},a_{2}))

\textstyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{y_{1},y_{2}\in B_{1,k},a_{1}\neq a_{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad

(64)

\textstyle g_{Z}((z_{1},b_{1}),(z_{2},b_{2}))

\textstyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{z_{1},z_{2}\in B_{2,k},b_{1}\neq b_{2}}

(65)

has power at least
1-\beta
whenever

\textstyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\Pi-\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q}}}}\right\|_{2}

\textstyle\geq\frac{C\,\sqrt[4]{L}}{\sqrt{\beta\alpha^{\star}}}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}}}\right)^{{2\tau/}{(4\tau+d_{1}+d_{2})}},

(66)

for a universal constant
C

and
\alpha^{\star}\triangleq\frac{\alpha}{2e}\big(\frac{\beta}{3}\big)^{1/\lfloor{\alpha(B+1)}\rfloor}
.

Remark 10

(Cheap optimality: Hölder
L^{2}
independence)
.

The cheap test of
Proposition

6
achieves the minimax optimal separation rate of order
n^{-2\tau/(4\tau+d_{1}+d_{2})}

[
31
, Prop. 5.6]

for
any
choice of
s
.

5.6
HSIC independence testing

Recently,
Kim and Schrab [33, Thm. S.4]
established a lower-bound of order
n^{-1/2}
on the minimax-optimal detectable separation in the (square-root) Hilbert-Schmidt independence criterion,
\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}(\Pi,\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q})
(
Example

3
), when
\Pi
is a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the positive-definite kernels
g_{Y}
and
g_{Z}
are bounded, translation-invariant, and non-constant.
To show that cheap independence testing can recover this optimal rate, we first establish two refined guarantees for testing with positive-definite kernels.

When the base functions
(g_{Y},g_{Z})
are positive-definite kernels,
the scaled independence V-statistic
\frac{1}{4}V_{n}
(
Def.

3
) is equal to the squared sample MMD,
\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}(\widehat{\Pi},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Q}})
.
In this case, the proof of
Thm.

2
also yields a detectable separation threshold of order
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
for the population parameter,
\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}(\Pi,\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q})
.

Corollary 3

(Power of cheap independence: finite variance, PD)
.

Under the assumptions of
Thm.

2
and notation of
Example

3
, suppose that
n\geq 14
and that
g_{Y},g_{Z}
are positive-definite kernels with

\mathbf{k}=g_{Y}g_{Z}
,

\xi\triangleq\mathbb{E}[h_{\mathrm{in}}(X_{1},X_{1},X_{3},X_{4})]
, and

\textstyle\tilde{\xi}_{\textup{wild}}\triangleq\max\big\{\mathbb{E}_{(Y,Z)\sim\mu}\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}\big(\frac{\delta_{(Y,Z)}+\nu}{2},\frac{\delta_{Y}\times\mathbb{Q}+\mathbb{P}\times\delta_{Z}}{2}\big):\mu,\nu\in\{\Pi,\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q}\}\big\}.

(67)

A cheap independence test based on
V_{n}
has power at least
1-\beta
whenever

\textstyle\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}(\Pi,\mathbb{P}\!\times\!\mathbb{Q})\!\geq\!

(68)

\textstyle\epsilon_{n,s}\triangleq

\textstyle\frac{(3-\sqrt{(\frac{12}{\alpha^{\star}\beta}-2)\frac{32}{n}})\,\epsilon_{n}+\frac{\mathbf{1}_{s<n}}{\sqrt{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\!16\sqrt{(\frac{12}{\alpha^{*}\beta}-2)\tilde{\xi}_{\textup{wild}}(\frac{72}{s}+\frac{14400}{s^{2}})}+\sqrt[4]{(\frac{12}{\alpha^{\star}\beta}-2)\psi_{2}^{\prime}\frac{4147200}{s}}\sqrt{3-\sqrt{(\frac{12}{\alpha^{\star}\beta}-2)\frac{32}{n}}}}}\right)}{3-\mathbf{1}_{s<n}\frac{s+1}{s^{2}}-\sqrt{(\frac{12}{\alpha^{\star}\beta}-2)\frac{32}{n}(1+\mathbf{1}_{s<n}(\frac{72}{s}+\frac{14400}{s^{2}}))}}

(69)

\textstyle\quad\text{for}\quad\epsilon_{n}\triangleq\frac{\sqrt{(12(\alpha^{\star}\beta)^{-1}-2)(32\,\xi+256\,\tilde{\xi}_{\textup{wild}})}}{(3-\sqrt{(12(\alpha^{\star}\beta)^{-1}-2)\frac{32}{n}})\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\sqrt[4]{(12(\alpha^{\star}\beta)^{-1}-2)960\,\psi_{2}^{\prime}}+\sqrt{10\tilde{\xi}}}{(3-\sqrt{(12(\alpha^{\star}\beta)^{-1}-2)\frac{32}{n}})^{1/2}\sqrt{n}}.

(70)

Remark 11

(Equivalent MMD separation rates and thresholds)
.

We have
\epsilon_{n,s}=\Theta(\epsilon_{n})
for
any
choice of
s

and
\epsilon_{n,s}=\epsilon_{n}(1+o(1))
whenever
s=\omega(1)
.

The proof of
Corollary

3
can be found in
App.

F
.
Our next result establishes improved power guarantees for cheap independence testing with only logarithmic dependence on
\beta
and
\alpha^{\star}
whenever
\mathbf{k}
is bounded.

Theorem 4

(Power of cheap independence: bounded, PSD)
.

Under the assumptions of
Thm.

2
and notation of
Example

3
, suppose that
g_{Y},g_{Z}
are positive-definite kernels with

\mathbf{k}=g_{Y}g_{Z}
and
K\triangleq\sup_{x}|\mathbf{k}(x,x)|<\infty
.
If
\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\log(6/\alpha^{\star})}{s}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(6/\alpha^{\star})}{s}}<1
for
\alpha^{\star}=\frac{\alpha}{2e}\big(\frac{\beta}{3}\big)^{1/\lfloor{\alpha(B+1)}\rfloor}
, then
a cheap independence test based on
V_{n}
has power at least
1-\beta
whenever

\textstyle\mathrm{MMD}_{\mathbf{k}}(\Pi,\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q})

\textstyle\geq\frac{\sqrt{K}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(30+22\sqrt{\log({12}{/\beta})}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{7}{2}+\frac{1+12\log({3}{/\alpha^{\star}})}{6\sqrt{s}}+2\sqrt{\log({3}{/\alpha^{\star}})}}}\right)}{\sqrt{n}\,\big(\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\log(6/\alpha^{\star})}{s}-\sqrt{\frac{\log(6/\alpha^{\star})}{s}}\big)}.

(71)

Remark 12

(Cheap optimality: HSIC independence)
.

The cheap test of
Thm.

4
with a bounded, translation-invariant, and non-constant kernels
g_{Y}
and
g_{Z}
achieves the minimax optimal separation rate of order
n^{-1/2}

[
33
, Thm. S.4]

whenever
s\geq 5\log(6/\alpha^{\star})
.

Thm.

4
, proven in
App.

G
,
establishes that cheap independence testing powerfully detects bounded MMD separations of order
\frac{\sqrt{\log(1/\beta)\log(1/\alpha^{\star})}}{\sqrt{n}}

using only
s=\Theta(\log(1/\alpha^{\star}))

bins.
This represents an exponential improvement over the order

\frac{\sqrt{1/(\beta\alpha^{\star})}}{\sqrt{n}}
threshold of
Corollary

3
and recovers the minimax optimal separation rate of
[
33
, Thm. S.4]
with a constant bin count independent of the sample size.

For bounded kernels bounded below by
0
,
Kim and Schrab [33, Thm. S.3]
recently established a minimum separation rate upper bound of order
\frac{\sqrt{\max(\log(1/\beta),\log(1/\alpha))}}{\sqrt{n}}
using a standard permutation test with
B\geq\frac{6}{\alpha}\log(2/\beta)
permutations and
\Theta(Bn^{2})
permutation overhead.
By
Thm.

4
, cheap testing in this setting can detect order
\frac{\sqrt{\log(1/\beta)}\sqrt[4]{\log(1/\alpha)}}{\sqrt{n}}
separations using
B=\Omega(\frac{\log(3/\beta)}{\alpha\log(2e/\alpha)})
permutations and
s=\Theta(\log(1/\alpha))
bins. Hence, binned wild bootstrapping can match both the rate and the power dependence of state-of-the-art permutation guarantees while running in time independent of
n
.

6
Experiments

We now complement our methodological development and theoretical analysis with a series of synthetic experiments exploring the practical benefits of cheap testing.
We implemented all tests in Cython using nominal level
\alpha=0.05
and a common interface and library of functions to enable a consistent comparison of computational complexity.
For each experiment, we display average rejection rates and
95\%
Wilson confidence intervals over
2500
independent replications with
B=299
permutations for Higgs boson testing and
10000
independent replications with
B=1279
permutations otherwise.
Python and Cython code replicating all experiments can be found at
https://github.com/microsoft/cheap-permutations
, and supplementary experimental details can be found in
App.

K
.

6.1
Homogeneity testing

We benchmark the power and runtime of homogeneity U-statistic (
Def.

2
) tests with
n_{1}=n_{2}
in three settings.

1.

MMD testing
:

\mathbb{P}=\mathcal{N}(-0.03e_{1},\mathrm{I})
and
\mathbb{Q}=\mathcal{N}(0.03e_{1},\mathrm{I})
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
d=10
, and
the base function
g
is the Gaussian kernel
\mathbf{k}_{\lambda}(x,y)\triangleq e^{-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x-y}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{/(2\lambda^{2})}}
with bandwidth
\lambda
equal to the median pairwise distance among sample points.

2.

RFF testing
:
\mathbb{P}
,
\mathbb{Q}
, and
\lambda
are as above, and the base function is the
random Fourier feature

[
49
, RFF,]
kernel
g(x,y)=\frac{4}{r^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{r}\cos(\omega_{k}^{\top}x+b_{k})\cos(\omega_{k}^{\top}y+b_{k})

for
(\omega_{k},b_{k})
sampled i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}})\times\textnormal{Unif}(0,2\pi)
. In this case, because the kernel
g
has rank
r<n
, each test statistic can be computed in time
\Theta(dnr)
, and cheap permutation can be carried out in
\Theta(Bsr)
additional time with
\Theta(sr)
memory, as described in
Alg.

3
.

3.

Aggregated MMD testing
:
An alternative to selecting a single kernel MMD for testing is to construct an aggregated test across a weighted collection of MMD test statistics indexed by kernel choice. We use the permutation-based MMDAgg aggregation procedure of
Schrab et al. [52]
atop a uniformly-weighted collection of Gaussian kernel MMDs with bandwidths
\lambda\in\{2^{i}\lambda_{0}\}_{i=0}^{-4}
where
\lambda_{0}
represents the median pairwise distance among sample points.
Our data comes from the Higgs boson dataset of
Baldi, Sadowski and Whiteson [3]
where
\mathbb{Q}
is distribution over the
\phi
-momenta of the two leading jets for a signal process that produces Higgs bosons and
\mathbb{P}
is the corresponding distribution for a background process that does not produce Higgs bosons.

4.

WMW testing
:
\mathbb{P}=\mathcal{N}(-0.015,1)
,
\mathbb{Q}=\mathcal{N}(0.015,1)
, and our test statistic is the popular Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney (WMW) test statistic of
Example

5
.

Example 5

(Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney test statistic
[
63
,
41
]
)
.

The
Wilcoxon-Mann-Whitney test statistic
(also known as the Wilcoxon rank sum statistic and the Mann-Whitney U-statistic) is defined as
T(\mathbb{Y},\mathbb{Z})=\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}\mathbf{1}_{Y_{i}\leq Z_{j}}-\frac{1}{2}
, where
\mathbb{Y}
and
\mathbb{Z}
are assumed to have no elements in common. This statistic may be recast into the U-statistic form (
5
) by selecting

\textstyle g(y,z)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{y<z}-\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{1}_{y=z}.

(72)

Technically, the WMW statistic is an
asymmetric
homogeneity U-statistic as
g(y,z)\neq g(z,y)
. Nevertheless, it remains a quadratic test statistic (
Def.

1
) and hence is amenable to cheap testing.

Figure 1:
Power vs. runtime for cheap and standard MMD tests of homogeneity
as total sample size
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and bin count
s
vary.

6.1.1
Cheap vs. standard MMD testing

Fig.

1
(left) compares cheap MMD testing to standard MMD permutation testing
as a function of the total sample size
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and bin count
s
.
We find that
s=128
bins suffice to closely match the power of standard testing for higher power levels (
1-\beta\geq 0.3
) and that
s=32
bins suffice for smaller power levels.
In both cases, cheap testing is consistently
100
times faster than the standard permutation test.

Fig.

1
(right) traces out the power-vs.-runtime trajectory of cheap testing for varying
s
and fixed
n=16384
.
We find that runtime remains essentially constant for
s\leq 128
and that power remains essentially constant for
s\geq 128
.

Figure 2:
Cheap MMD vs. asymptotic cross-MMD tests of homogeneity.
(Left) Power as a function of the total sample size
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
.
(Right) Time-power trade-off curves as
n
and bin count
s
vary.

6.1.2
Cheap MMD vs. asymptotic cross-MMD testing

A recently-proposed alternative to the MMD permutation test is the
asymptotic cross-MMD test

[
56
]
which avoids permutation by thresholding a related, asymptotically-normal test statistic using a Gaussian quantile.
By avoiding permutation, the asymptotic cross-MMD test achieves a substantial speed-up over a standard MMD permutation test. However, as
Shekhar, Kim and Ramdas [56]
note, this comes at the cost of reduced power and the loss of finite-sample validity.
In
Fig.

2
, we see that cheap MMD achieves nearly the same speed-up benefits as asymptotic cross-MMD (right) while also retaining the full power and final-sample validity of standard MMD testing for any given sample size (left).

Figure 3:
Power vs. runtime for cheap and standard MMDAgg tests (left) and MMD tests (right) of homogeneity
as total sample size
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and bin count
s
vary.

6.1.3
Cheap vs. standard aggregated MMD testing

Fig.

3
(left) compares cheap MMDAgg to standard MMDAgg
as a function of the total sample size
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and bin count
s
.
We find that
s=32
bins suffice to closely match the power of standard testing for all power levels. With this setting, cheap testing is consistently
100
times faster than the standard MMDAgg permutation test.

Fig.

3
(right) highlights the primary benefit of aggregated MMD testing: testing the same data with a single kernel bandwidth, like the popular popular median bandwidth
\lambda_{0}
, results in a substantial degradation in power for standard testing and a matched degradation for cheap testing, albeit in
100\times
less time.

6.1.4
Cheap vs. standard RFF testing

Fig.

4
compares cheap RFF testing to standard RFF permutation testing
as a function of the number of random Fourier features
r
and bin count
s
.
We find that
s=256
bins suffice to closely match the power of standard testing for all power levels.
With this setting, cheap testing is also
100
to
1000
times faster than the standard permutation test, depending on the choice of
r
.

Figure 4:
Power vs. runtime for cheap and standard RFF tests of homogeneity
as the bin count
s
and number of random Fourier features
r
vary. Here, the total sample size is
n=16384
.

Cheap vs. standard WMW testing

Fig.

5
(left) compares cheap WMW testing to standard WMW permutation testing
as a function of the total sample size
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and bin count
s
.
We find that
s=128
bins suffice to closely match the power of standard testing for higher power levels (
1-\beta\geq 0.3
) and that
s=64
bins suffice for smaller power levels.
In both cases, cheap testing is consistently
300
to
900
times faster than the standard permutation test.

Fig.

5
(right) traces out the power-vs.-runtime trajectory of cheap testing for varying
s
and fixed
n=16384
.
We find that runtime remains essentially constant for
s\leq 64
and that power increases minimally beyond
s=128
.

We additionally benchmark the power and runtime of independence V-statistic (
Def.

3
) tests in the following setting.

4.

HSIC testing
:

\Pi
a multivariate Gaussian distribution with mean zero and covariance

\textstyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{I}&0.047\mathrm{I}\\
0.047\mathrm{I}&\mathrm{I}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{20\times 20}.

(73)

The base functions
g_{Y}
and
g_{Z}
are Gaussian kernels
\mathbf{k}_{\lambda_{Y}}
and
\mathbf{k}_{\lambda_{Z}}
with bandwidths
\lambda_{Y}
and
\lambda_{Z}
equal to the median pairwise distance among
\mathbb{Y}
and
\mathbb{Z}
points respectively.

6.2.1
Cheap vs. standard HSIC testing

Fig.

6
(left) compares cheap HSIC testing to standard HSIC wild bootstrap testing
as a function of the total sample size
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and bin count
s
.
We find that
s=128
bins suffice to closely match the power of standard testing for higher power levels (
1-\beta\geq 0.3
) and that
s=64
bins suffice for smaller power levels.
In both cases, cheap testing is consistently
15
times faster than the standard wild bootstrap test.

Fig.

(right) traces out the power-vs.-runtime trajectory of cheap testing for varying
s
and fixed
n=2048
.
We find that runtime remains essentially constant for

and that power increases minimally beyond

.
